     1	---
     2	name: theming
     3	description: >-
     4	  Use when semantic roles must map coherently across light, dark, forced-colors, or
     5	  saved theme preferences without contrast or no-flash regressions.
     6	---
     7	
     8	# Theming
     9	
    10	A theme system keeps component roles stable while their values change. Components
    11	reference semantic tokens such as `--color-bg`, `--color-text`, and `--color-focus`;
    12	supported light, dark, or branded modes remap those roles without rewriting components.
    13	
    14	This skill builds that system. The hard part isn't the control — it is tuning each
    15	supported mapping in context. Dark is not inverted light, an authored high-contrast theme
    16	is not forced colors, and no mode earns an accessibility claim without measurement.
    17	
    18	## The decision order
    19	1. **Semantic tokens first.** Define roles (`--color-bg`, `--color-surface-1/2` (`-3` if elevation needs it),
    20	   `--color-text`, `--color-text-muted`, `--color-border`, `--color-primary`,
    21	   `--color-primary-hover`, `--color-primary-ink`, `--color-accent`, `--color-accent-ink`,
    22	   `--color-success`, `--color-error`, `--color-warning`, `--color-info`,
    23	   `--color-focus` — the canonical contract names). Components
    24	   use these only. (Palette comes from `color-system`.)
    25	2. **Choose the product baseline.** Start with the application's default supported mode;
    26	   it may be light, dark, or brand-specific. Set structure and role relationships there.
    27	3. **Tune every additional mapping**, never invert it — for dark, a surface *ramp* (each elevation step
    28	   lighter, not shadowed), comfortable text contrast on dark surfaces, and accents
    29	   retuned for the surrounding lightness. (Depth in `references/surfaces-and-elevation.md` +
    30	   `color-and-contrast.md`.)
    31	4. **Keep authored contrast and forced colors separate.** `prefers-contrast: more` may
    32	   select an authored higher-contrast mapping. `forced-colors` is a system-controlled
    33	   environment: honor system colors instead of treating it as another palette.
    34	5. **Resolve preferences deliberately.** Use `prefers-color-scheme` when applicable;
    35	   add a persistent user control when the product supports selectable themes. Set
    36	   `color-scheme` so native controls follow.
    37	6. **Verify each theme** — contrast on the *actual* surfaces of *each* theme.
    38	
    39	## Vary the mappings, preserve the contract
    40	
    41	The starter values demonstrate role mapping and preference order, not a dark-theme
    42	recipe. Derive each mapping from the supplied semantic roles, ambient conditions,
    43	brand direction, and platform constraints. A credible theme system may use quiet
    44	surfaces, high-information field surfaces, or a more editorial reading surface, but it
    45	must keep role names, forced-colors respect, preference precedence, and measured pairs
    46	intact. When source roles are missing, stop and name the recovery needed rather than
    47	inventing raw values in components.
    48	
    49	## Non-negotiables
    50	- **One semantic-token source; components never hard-code colors.** Re-theming = remap
    51	  tokens, nothing else.
    52	- **Choose light surfaces from context.** Pure or softened endpoints can both be valid;
    53	  test contrast, glare conditions, reading comfort, and user preferences rather than
    54	  attaching one palette recipe to a diagnosis.
    55	- **Dark is tuned, not inverted:** near-black surfaces are a reliable default, but pure
    56	  black can be intentional for OLED, projection, or a stark visual system. Express
    57	  elevation through distinguishable surfaces or borders, tune text below glare-inducing
    58	  white where appropriate, and remeasure every accent in context.
    59	- **Ship a defined default and resolution policy.** If users can select a theme, their
    60	  supported choice persists and outranks OS preference. A single-theme product does
    61	  not need a performative toggle.
    62	- **`color-scheme` declared** per theme (native controls/scrollbars follow).
    63	- **Respect `forced-colors`/high-contrast** — don't override system colors there; use
    64	  `forced-color-adjust` only deliberately, keep focus visible.
    65	- **Contrast re-verified per theme** (body ≥4.5, large/UI ≥3) against that theme's
    66	  surfaces — a color that passes in light often fails in dark, and vice-versa.
    67	
    68	## Theme resolution and recovery
    69	
    70	Separate three decisions that are often accidentally merged: stable semantic role names,
    71	the values each supported theme maps to those roles, and the policy that chooses a theme
    72	at render time. Document the precedence in plain language: system forced-colors takes
    73	authority; then an explicit supported user choice; then the operating-system preference;
    74	then the application default. When a theme control exists, define its precedence,
    75	storage behavior, and no-preference fallback.
    76	
    77	Treat forced-colors as an environment, not another palette. Let system colors and native
    78	control behavior remain authoritative, and preserve warnings, errors, selections, and
    79	focus with text, icons, state, or shape as well as color. If a semantic role is absent,
    80	record a failed recovery row and stop the mapping decision; reusing a convenient accent
    81	silently changes the role’s meaning.
    82	
    83	Minimize wrong-theme flash at the earliest theme application point. State what happens
    84	before the first paint, when a saved choice is unavailable, and how native controls
    85	receive the matching color-scheme. A preference plan is not a verified no-flash result
    86	until a cold-load observation names the theme, artifact, and timing.
    87	
    88	## Quick-start
    89	
    90	Use `assets/theme-starter.css` as a mapping example. Preserve the invariants that apply
    91	to the supported scope: semantic roles, tuned authored mappings, `forced-colors`
    92	cooperation, and early preference resolution when a saved choice exists.
    93	
    94	**Early saved-theme resolution:** when a persistent control exists, apply the validated
    95	override before render. In a CSP-compatible implementation, this is commonly a small
    96	nonced or hashed `<head>` script rather than a deferred bundle:
    97	
    98	```html
    99	<script>/* inline, in <head>, before CSS paint */
   100	  try {
   101	    const t = localStorage.getItem("theme");
   102	    if (t === "dark" || t === "light") document.documentElement.dataset.theme = t;
   103	  } catch {
   104	    /* Storage is unavailable; CSS keeps the OS/application default authoritative. */
   105	  }
   106	</script>
   107	```
   108	
   109	Update supported browser chrome where it materially improves the experience. Treat
   110	selection and custom scrollbar styling as optional polish, not completion gates.
   111	### `light-dark()` shorthand
   112	
   113	When `color-scheme` drives the switch (no persisted override), `light-dark()` avoids
   114	media-query duplication: `background: light-dark(oklch(96% .02 78), oklch(18% .024 64))`.
   115	Broad browser support (2024+); pair with `[data-theme]` so explicit user choice and
   116	forced-colors still override. Not the sole mechanism when a manual toggle exists.
   117	
   118	## Reference files
   119	- `references/surfaces-and-elevation.md` — the **dark** surface ramp + elevation-by-
   120	  lightness (Material overlay model), borders, why shadows fail on dark.
   121	- `references/color-and-contrast.md` — desaturating accents for dark, semantic state
   122	  colors per theme, contrast re-testing, OKLCH conversions.
   123	- `references/light-and-contrast.md` — contextual light mappings, surface relationships,
   124	  accent contrast, and rendered checks.
   125	- `references/high-contrast.md` — `prefers-contrast` + `forced-colors`/Windows High
   126	  Contrast: what to do and what NOT to override.
   127	- `references/decision-records.md` — meta-patterns + ADR rules.
   128	
   129	## Completion evidence
   130	
   131	Close with a five-field evidence ledger. Put the check ID at the start of Reason and
   132	name the environment or mode in Evidence.
   133	
   134	| Status | Reason | Remediation | Evidence | Provenance |
   135	| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   136	|  | role-map — semantic roles across supported themes |  |  |  |
   137	|  | supported-maps — each requested mode is tuned rather than inverted |  |  |  |
   138	|  | resolution — preference, no-flash, and native-control behavior |  |  |  |
   139	|  | forced-colors — high-contrast environmental behavior |  |  |  |
   140	|  | contrast-handoff — per-theme proof and accessibility boundary |  |  |  |
   141	
   142	## How to deliver
   143	
   144	Report semantic roles, supported mappings, resolution policy, wrong-theme-flash behavior,
   145	and per-theme contrast. Include task-level proof for the environments in scope and
   146	forced-colors where applicable. Hand source ramps, type, technical
   147	a11y, and cognitive concerns to the adjacent skills. Provenance is credited and
   148	independent.
   149	
   150	<!-- contract:v1:start -->

   152	
   153	Canonical detail: [contract.json](contract.json).
   154	
   155	- Route: An interface needs coherent light, dark, high-contrast, or forced-colors mappings (+1 in contract.json); avoid: The request is to invent the source palette rather than map semantic roles (+1 in contract.json)
   156	- Exclude: Do not invert a light palette and call it dark mode (+1 in contract.json)
   157	- Stop / handoff: Pause when semantic roles are missing and theme values would be raw-color copies (+1 in contract.json); receives [color-system, design-system-interview, improve-existing-website, tasteroll] -> sends [a11y-pass, cognitive-a11y, responsive-layout, component-states]
   158	- Output: Semantic mappings for every supported theme, plus forced-colors behavior, preference policy, and measured pairs

     1	---
     2	name: responsive-layout
     3	description: >-
     4	  Use when a page or component must survive narrow containers, long or translated
     5	  content, zoom, reflow, or overflow without device-name breakpoints.
     6	---
     7	
     8	# Responsive Layout
     9	
    10	Responsive layout is intrinsic reflow plus content-led breakpoints and container queries,
    11	not named-device CSS.
    12	
    13	## The decision order
    14	
    15	1. Start mobile-first and use Grid/Flex intrinsic sizing (`minmax`, `auto-fit`, `clamp`, `%`/`fr`).
    16	2. Add breakpoints only where observed content breaks; reusable components use container queries.
    17	3. Verify the product's minimum supported viewport, 200–400% zoom, long/translated
    18	   content, narrow embeds, and wide views without accidental overflow or collapse.
    19	
    20	## Preserve hierarchy under pressure
    21	
    22	Start with non-disappearing content, reading order, and narrowest real container; decide
    23	stack/reorder/progressive reveal/rail from observed pressure and test long content, locale,
    24	zoom, and embedded cases before calling it responsive.
    25	
    26	## Non-negotiables
    27	
    28	- Start from the narrowest supported content state; avoid device-name breakpoints.
    29	- Prevent overflow with `min-width: 0`, bounded media, and wrapping; reflow rather than shrink to unusable.
    30	- Prefer container queries for reusable components when their behavior depends on their
    31	  container, not the viewport.
    32	- At narrow widths and zoom, ordinary reading and interaction should not require
    33	  two-dimensional scrolling. Isolate essential exceptions such as wide data tables,
    34	  maps, timelines, or canvases in a labelled, keyboard-operable scroll region.
    35	
    36	## Quick-start patterns
    37	
    38	Use `references/patterns.md` for the intrinsic grid, sidebar, stack, cluster, and
    39	container-query patterns. Start with `minmax(min(16rem, 100%), 1fr)`, `min-width: 0`,
    40	logical container sizing, and a breakpoint only where content actually breaks.
    41	
    42	## Reference files
    43	
    44	- `assets/layout-patterns.css` — intrinsic examples after pressure evidence.
    45	- `references/patterns.md` — patterns, units, images, and overflow debugging.
    46	- `references/decision-records.md` — novel-case ADR rules.
    47	
    48	## Completion evidence
    49	
    50	Use the five-field ledger; start Reason with the check ID.
    51	
    52	| Status | Reason | Remediation | Evidence | Provenance |
    53	| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
    54	|  | layout-strategy — content hierarchy and intrinsic composition |  |  |  |
    55	|  | containment — breakpoint and container-query rationale |  |  |  |
    56	|  | narrow-reflow — 320px, long-content, and overflow result |  |  |  |
    57	|  | zoom-interaction — zoom and keyboard-reachable interaction result |  |  |  |
    58	|  | handoff — component and accessibility boundary |  |  |  |
    59	
    60	## Pressure-test protocol
    61	
    62	Each row names real longest-pressure fixture, available width/zoom, expected and observed
    63	result (or fail-closed missing evidence). Test full shell, narrow, 400%, and narrow embed;
    64	record what stays first/stacks/folds and why. No rendered build means a plan, not pass.
    65	
    66	## How to deliver
    67	
    68	Deliver content-led layout/breakpoints and rerunnable narrow/long/container/zoom/keyboard
    69	evidence; hand type, rhythm, and state concerns onward.
    70

     1	---
     2	name: component-states
     3	description: >-
     4	  State matrix for interactive UI. Use when building or reviewing buttons, links,
     5	  inputs, tabs, toggles, menus, cards, or controls that need hover, focus, active,
     6	  disabled, loading, selected, error, keyboard, or ARIA states.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Component States
    10	
    11	An interactive control is a lifecycle, not a resting style. Map every applicable state
    12	to semantics, a visible treatment, and recovery.
    13	
    14	## Decision order and evidence contract
    15	
    16	1. Name role, interaction model, owner, and applicable states.
    17	2. Give each state an ARIA/DOM mapping, visible non-color treatment, trigger, and recovery.
    18	3. Test keyboard, focus-visible, contrast, reduced motion, and async completion.
    19	
    20	Report one evidence row per state; `n/a` needs a subject-absence reason. Hand field
    21	validation to `form-ux`, region emptiness to `empty-states`, and missing state tokens
    22	back as gaps rather than invented fallbacks.
    23	
    24	## Async and destructive controls
    25	
    26	Name one semantic owner; visual states only present its lifecycle.
    27	
    28	| State / event | Guard and observable contract |
    29	|---|---|
    30	| `ready` / invoke | Click, Enter, Space, and script use one eligible route; accepted work creates a request key. |
    31	| `confirming` | Use only for irreversible, broad, or cascading work; name consequence, move focus in, and restore the invoker on cancel. |
    32	| `submitting` | Create the key before dispatch; reject duplicates; expose native disabled/busy and a working label. |
    33	| matching completion | Only the matching key may settle current work; stale results cannot clear busy, alter another target, or move focus. |
    34	| success / failure | Record data before presentation; confirm locally and choose focus, or retain context with nearby error and retry/cancel. |
    35	
    36	Test a real and a stale/failed completion. If success empties a region, hand that
    37	region to `empty-states`.
    38	
    39	## The universal state matrix
    40	
    41	Every interactive element should account for these (not all apply to every component):
    42	
    43	| State | Trigger | Must communicate | CSS |
    44	|-------|---------|------------------|-----|
    45	| **Default** | rest | "this is here / interactive" | base styles |
    46	| **Hover** | pointer over | "you can interact" (pointer devices only) | `:hover` |
    47	| **Focus** | keyboard tab / programmatic | "this is where you are" (keyboard) | `:focus-visible` |
    48	| **Active / pressed** | during click/tap | "your action registered" | `:active` |
    49	| **Disabled** | not available | "not available, and why" | `:disabled` / `[aria-disabled]` |
    50	| **Loading** | async in progress | "working, wait" | a `data-loading` / `aria-busy` class |
    51	| **Selected / current** | chosen / active page | "this is the active one" | `[aria-selected]`/`[aria-current]` |
    52	| **Error / invalid** | failed validation | "something's wrong here" | `[aria-invalid]` |
    53	
    54	The first five are the baseline for *any* control. Add selected (tabs, nav, options)
    55	and error (form fields) where they apply.
    56	
    57	## Non-negotiables
    58	
    59	- Never ship default-only: applicable hover, focus-visible, active, disabled, loading,
    60	  selected, and error states need semantics and non-color signals.
    61	- Never remove a focus outline without an equally visible replacement; hover is not focus.
    62	- Explain unavailability; a silent disabled submit is a `form-ux` failure.
    63	- Async controls prevent duplicate dispatch, expose busy state, and preserve contrast
    64	  (≥3:1 UI, ≥4.5:1 text). Use fast reduced-motion-safe feedback.
    65	
    66	## Implementation starter
    67	
    68	Read `assets/states-starter.css` for tokenized CSS and reduced-motion details. A missing
    69	token is a gap, never a fallback hex; add the request guard before styling async work.
    70	
    71	## State by component (what each one needs)
    72	
    73	- **Button/link:** default, hover, focus-visible, active, disabled/loading as applicable;
    74	  toggles use `aria-pressed`, links retain a non-color cue/visited treatment.
    75	- **Toggle / switch:** use `role="switch"` (not `role="checkbox"`) with `aria-checked`;
    76	  Space toggles, Tab moves focus. Example: `<button role="switch" aria-checked="true">Notifications</button>`.
    77	  Screen readers announce "switch, on/off" instead of "checkbox, checked."
    78	- **Fields:** focus, disabled, filled/readonly, error, and selected/open/checked states;
    79	  style a real or correctly labelled focusable input (`form-ux` owns validation).
    80	- **Navigation/list/menu:** current/selected state uses `aria-current`/`aria-selected`;
    81	  pointer and keyboard focus are equally legible.
    82	- **Overlays:** model open/closed and entry/exit; prefer native Popover or `<dialog>`;
    83	  toasts use `role="status"` and hover-only content remains reachable by focus.
    84	
    85	## Reference files
    86	
    87	- `references/states.md` — state semantics, pitfalls, and ARIA pairings.
    88	- `references/decision-records.md` — novel-case ADR rules.
    89	
    90	## Self-check (per interactive element)
    91	
    92	For each applicable state, confirm visible focus, semantics, non-color contrast, and
    93	reduced-motion behavior. For async/destructive work confirm owner, guard, justified
    94	confirmation, duplicate prevention, recovery, and final focus; exercise real and stale
    95	or failed completion separately. Do not collapse controls into one checkmark.
    96	
    97	## How to deliver
    98	
    99	Deliver the full applicable matrix plus lifecycle evidence. Pair with `form-ux`,
   100	`empty-states`, `micro-motion`, `a11y-pass`, and `color-system` at their boundaries.
   101

   103	## Contract (generated)
   104	
   105	Canonical detail: [contract.json](contract.json).
   106	
   107	- Route: A control has interaction states missing or inconsistent with its semantics. (+1 in contract.json); avoid: The request is only about form copy or validation timing. (+1 in contract.json)
   108	- Exclude: Do not invent page-level empty states or validation policy. (+2 in contract.json)
   109	- Stop / handoff: Stop when the component semantics or interactive states cannot be identified. (+2 in contract.json); receives [design-system-interview, empty-states, improve-existing-website, responsive-layout, spacing-system, tasteroll, theming] -> sends [form-ux, empty-states, a11y-pass, micro-motion, tastecheck-pass]
   110	- Output: component lifecycle contract and implementation guidance
   111	- Evidence: `table_with_evidence` with `status`, `reason`, `remediation`, `evidence`, `provenance`.

     1	---
     2	name: form-ux
     3	description: >-
     4	  Form UX and accessibility pass. Use for forms, inputs, checkout, signup/login,
     5	  validation, labels, autocomplete, mobile keyboards, inline errors, required
     6	  fields, disabled submits, and field-level accessibility.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Form UX
    10	
    11	A form is a completion path: reduce effort, prevent predictable errors, and make every
    12	failure truthful, repairable, and accessible.
    13	
    14	## Model the task before styling fields
    15	
    16	Start with outcome, risk, and data authority—not an inherited input array. Remove,
    17	defer, or explain each field through this contract:
    18	
    19	| Field contract | What to decide |
    20	| --- | --- |
    21	| User value | What completing this field unlocks now, in the user's words |
    22	| Data rule | Required/optional condition, source of truth, and whether the rule is client- or server-owned |
    23	| Input affordance | Label, type, inputmode, autocomplete, format help, and a safe example |
    24	| Validation moment | What can be checked while editing, on blur, on submit, or only after a server request |
    25	| Recovery | Exact message, preserved value, focus destination, and next available action |
    26	| Sensitivity | Whether the field is personal, financial, irreversible, or must never be persisted locally |
    27	
    28	Unknown rule/ownership stays unresolved; never invent validation. Group by the user’s
    29	task, not database/API shape.
    30	
    31	## Non-negotiables
    32	
    33	- Persistent associated label; visible required/optional state; correct type, inputmode,
    34	  autocomplete, and a one-column task flow (short related pairs excepted).
    35	- Validate on submit. Validate on blur only when the value is complete enough to judge;
    36	  after an error, re-check while the person repairs it. Keep server truth server-owned.
    37	- Put specific, adjacent “what + fix” errors behind `aria-invalid`/`aria-describedby`;
    38	  announce and focus first error on failed submit.
    39	- Never silently disable submit; retain visible focus and explain what is missing.
    40	
    41	## The error-message formula
    42	
    43	Use `[what is wrong] + [how to fix it] (+ example)`, plainly and without blame. Tie it
    44	visually/programmatically to the field; a top summary links to inline errors on long forms.
    45	
    46	## Validation is a conversation, not a trap
    47	
    48	Give feedback at the earliest truthful moment; browser checks never impersonate server
    49	truth. On submit distinguish field errors (focus/retain), conflict (correct branch),
    50	transient failure (preserved draft/safe retry), authorization (boundary/route), and
    51	irreversible payment (duplicate lock plus receipt/status). Pending work has an accessible
    52	label/status and rejects duplicates; failure restores a usable explained route. Cancel or
    53	ignore stale asynchronous validation responses so an older result cannot overwrite the
    54	current value.
    55	
    56	### Worked field contract
    57	
    58	| Field | Rule owner | When to check | Repair path |
    59	| --- | --- | --- | --- |
    60	| Email | server owns account availability; client owns basic shape | submit; blur after a complete address; re-check while repairing | “Enter an address like name@example.com.” Preserve the value; announce an account conflict only after the server responds. |
    61	
    62	The row separates a local affordance from server authority. Apply that distinction to
    63	cross-field rules, invitation codes, inventory, pricing, and payment status.
    64	
    65	## Quick-start
    66	
    67	Use `references/patterns.md` for markup/wiring; retain the field contract above.
    68	
    69	## Reduce effort (completion-rate wins)
    70	
    71	Ask only necessary fields; defer optional data. Use safe defaults/autofill, helpful
    72	nonblocking formatting, password reveal/paste, inline help, preserved input, and saved
    73	progress for long flows.
    74	
    75	## Reference files
    76	
    77	- `references/patterns.md` — types, validation, mobile/autofill, and a11y wiring.
    78	- `references/decision-records.md` — novel-case ADR rules.
    79	
    80	## Decision order and evidence
    81	
    82	Establish task, fields, rule owner, success/risk/interruption before styling. Record each
    83	field/state plus validation owner/recovery; `n/a` needs subject absence, never an unknown rule.
    84	
    85	## Self-check (before shipping a form)
    86	
    87	1. Field contract, labels, required state, types/autofill, and keyboard/screen-reader wiring complete?
    88	2. Submit/blur/repair timing, stale async responses, specific adjacent errors, first-error focus, and retained input work?
    89	3. No silent disabled submit; pending, duplicate, conflict, authorization, and irreversible outcomes differ?
    90	
    91	## How to deliver
    92	
    93	Deliver one field/state matrix with source rule, affordance, owner/timing, recovery,
    94	pending behavior, evidence, and remaining risk; hand adjacent concerns to their skills.
    95	
    96	<!-- contract:v1:start -->
    97	## Contract (generated)
    98	
    99	Canonical detail: [contract.json](contract.json).
   100	
   101	- Route: A form has completion, validation, recovery, autofill, or interruption problems.; avoid: The issue is only a control's visual interaction state.
   102	- Exclude: Do not hide errors or validate only on blur without a recovery path. (+1 in contract.json)
   103	- Stop / handoff: Stop when validation rules or data ownership are unknown. (+1 in contract.json); receives [component-states, design-system-interview, deslop-ui, humanize-copy, responsive-layout, spacing-system, tasteroll] -> sends [a11y-pass, i18n-ready, tastecheck-pass, cognitive-a11y]
   104	- Output: completion-oriented form flow and evidence table
   105	- Evidence: `table_with_evidence` with `status`, `reason`, `remediation`, `evidence`, `provenance`.

     1	---
     2	name: empty-states
     3	description: >-
     4	  Empty, loading, and error-state design. Use for first-run screens, zero results,
     5	  empty lists/tables/dashboards, loading skeletons, offline/permission errors,
     6	  retries, layout stability, and state copy.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Empty States
    10	
    11	Every reachable data state needs a truthful treatment; absence and failure are different
    12	conditions with different safe next moves.
    13	
    14	## Start with a state contract, not a set of illustrations
    15	
    16	Name the region and transition before copy or illustration. Record:
    17	
    18	| Question | Decision to record |
    19	| --- | --- |
    20	| User goal | What they came to find, create, compare, or finish in this region |
    21	| Data authority | Network source, local draft, cached result, permission boundary, or user-entered filter |
    22	| Entry condition | The observable event that enters loading, zero-result, error, stale, or partial state |
    23	| Continuity | What remains visible and editable while the state is active |
    24	| Recovery | The action that can change the state, its owner, and whether retry is safe to repeat |
    25	| Exit proof | The event that replaces the state and the announcement/visual confirmation the user receives |
    26	
    27	First-use, zero results, and permission absence need different truth. Trusted cached data
    28	is stale content with progress—not a blank reset.
    29	
    30	## Model the reachable state set
    31	
    32	Design loading when work can be in flight, empty when a successful result can contain
    33	zero items, and error when the region can fail. A static or fully local region need not
    34	invent unreachable states. Add stale, partial, awaiting-input, or pending-mutation only
    35	when their entry condition exists; never render zero, unavailable, and loading as the same state.
    36	
    37	## Loading: communicate the wait honestly
    38	
    39	Use a layout-matching skeleton when the final geometry is known and the placeholder will
    40	help orientation. Use determinate progress when progress is measurable, a compact status
    41	for short inline waits, and a spinner only as a supporting cue. Delay transient indicators
    42	when measured latency makes flashing likely, but keep slow work visibly alive. Never imply
    43	progress or shape the system cannot know. Use optimistic UI only when rollback and
    44	reconciliation are safe.
    45	
    46	## Empty: the three flavors, each with a next step
    47	
    48	Every empty state has heading, context, and a safe way forward. First-run teaches the
    49	first action; user-cleared affirms completion; no-results names the query/filter and
    50	offers clear/broaden/correct/create. If no action exists, say what will fill it.
    51	
    52	## Error: explain, reassure, offer recovery
    53	
    54	Use plain, blameless language; distinguish offline, permission, not-found, and server
    55	when recovery differs. Preserve work and offer retry, a working route, or support with
    56	a tone proportionate to the consequence.
    57	
    58	## Preserve continuity through transitions
    59	
    60	Retain position, prior content, filters, and drafts unless safety/integrity forbids it.
    61	Make in-flight retry visible without duplicating mutation; explain rollback and return
    62	focus to repair. Record idempotent, confirmation-required, or support-only retry—never
    63	promise retry can resolve permission or duplicate-money risk. Give each request an owner:
    64	late responses from an abandoned query or retry must not replace the current state.
    65	
    66	## Quick-start pattern
    67	
    68	Use `references/patterns.md` for state-specific markup and copy. Render one explicit
    69	state per region, reserve the same container space, announce changes, and preserve
    70	user work across errors.
    71	
    72	## Reference files
    73	
    74	- `references/patterns.md` — surface patterns, copy, skeletons, and announcements.
    75	- `references/decision-records.md` — novel-case ADR rules.
    76	
    77	## Decision order and evidence
    78	
    79	For each applicable state, record cause, retained context, next action, retry semantics,
    80	exit proof, and accessible recovery; `n/a` requires subject absence. Hand control
    81	behavior to `component-states`.
    82	
    83	## Self-check (before shipping any data region)
    84	
    85	1. Every reachable loading, empty, and error state is distinct and truthful?
    86	2. Empty has heading/context/forward action; no-results has an exit?
    87	3. Errors explain/recover without raw internals; loading feedback matches what is actually known?
    88	4. Changes announce, preserve work, reject stale responses, and state safe retry/cached/partial behavior?
    89	
    90	## How to deliver
    91	
    92	Deliver a region matrix: entry, truthful message, retained context, recovery, exit,
    93	announcement, and layout-shift evidence. Keep containers stable; hand adjacent scope off.
    94	
    95	<!-- contract:v1:start -->
    96	## Contract (generated)
    97	
    98	Canonical detail: [contract.json](contract.json).
    99	
   100	- Route: A data region needs first-use, no-results, loading, permission, or error states.; avoid: The issue is only a button or field state.
   101	- Exclude: Do not use a spinner as the only loading communication. (+1 in contract.json)
   102	- Stop / handoff: Stop when the cause of absence cannot be distinguished.      1	---
     2	name: micro-motion
     3	description: >-
     4	  Use when an interface needs purposeful feedback, transitions, or reduced-motion
     5	  behavior, especially when animation risks jank, interruption bugs, or hidden
     6	  no-JS content.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Micro-Motion
    10	
    11	Motion confirms a change, its origin, or what to notice next. If it answers none of
    12	those, cut it.
    13	
    14	## Non-negotiables (the rules that keep motion feeling good)
    15	
    16	- **Prefer compositor-safe properties.** Start with `transform` and `opacity`. Color,
    17	  filter, SVG, or measured layout transitions are valid when the purpose requires them
    18	  and a performance trace shows the target device can sustain the interaction.
    19	- **Ease by direction.** Ease-out enters, ease-in exits, ease-in-out moves; linear is
    20	  for a purposeful continuous loop only.
    21	- **Design reduced motion as an equivalent state.** Keep labels, choices, focus, and
    22	  final content; remove spatial movement.
    23	- **Let JavaScript opt individual elements into waiting states only after the observer
    24	  is ready.** Static CSS must not leave content hidden when JavaScript fails, initializes
    25	  late, or restores from the back-forward cache.
    26	- **Make looping motion controllable and never scroll-jack.**
    27	
    28	## Easing & duration tokens
    29	
    30	Use semantic duration/easing tokens: acknowledgement `120–160ms`, insertion/menu
    31	`160–220ms`, confirmation/route `220–320ms`. Treat these as starting bands and test on
    32	the target device. Motion never delays committed state, focus, or the next keyboard action.
    33	
    34	## Settle before styling
    35	
    36	Define the usable end state and interruption policy before choosing an entrance. A
    37	superseded save, route, insertion, or dismissal must converge on the newest state without
    38	a stale overlay, announcement, focus jump, or success message. Motion reflects state
    39	ownership; it does not decide it. If ownership or focus recovery is unknown, hand off to
    40	`component-states` before choreographing.
    41	
    42	## The reduced-motion contract
    43	
    44	Define the project-level reduced-motion policy once, then let components reference it.
    45	For progressive reveals, content starts visible. After the observer is attached,
    46	JavaScript marks only offscreen pending elements; never hide an element already
    47	intersecting the viewport. It removes the marker on reveal and on page restoration.
    48	Gate spatial movement behind `no-preference`:
    49	
    50	```css
    51	/* JavaScript marks only non-intersecting elements after the observer is ready. */
    52	.reveal[data-reveal="pending"] { opacity: 0; }
    53	.reveal:not([data-reveal="pending"]) { opacity: 1; }
    54	@media (prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference) {
    55	  .reveal[data-reveal="pending"] { transform: translateY(12px); }
    56	  .reveal { transition: opacity var(--dur-base) var(--ease-out),
    57	                        transform var(--dur-base) var(--ease-out); }
    58	  .reveal:not([data-reveal="pending"]) { transform: none; }
    59	}
    60	```
    61	
    62	For a legacy retrofit only, the emergency global kill switch in
    63	`references/principles.md` is permitted after documenting why the primary pattern cannot
    64	be used; it removes useful transitions, so it is not the default.
    65	
    66	## Decision order
    67	
    68	1. Name the user-facing purpose: acknowledgement, continuity, spatial origin, or attention.
    69	2. Name trigger, settled state, focus timing, and what wins when interrupted.
    70	3. Choose the smallest property, duration, and easing that communicate that purpose.
    71	4. Define reduced-motion and no-JS/restoration equivalents.
    72	5. Replay rapid repeat, cancellation, navigation, and stale async completion.
    73	
    74	For async feedback, tag each request and let only the current owner update status or its
    75	live announcement. For routes and overlays, cancel superseded animation and make the
    76	destination DOM and focus authoritative. For list changes, preserve readable DOM order
    77	even when visual movement is interrupted.
    78	
    79	## How to deliver
    80	
    81	Deliver a compact choreography table: interaction, purpose, trigger, duration/easing,
    82	settled state, interruption rule, and reduced equivalent. Add one global safety row for
    83	JS-disabled and restoration behavior. Default to CSS; use a library when gestures,
    84	springs, or layout coordination justify its runtime and ownership complexity.
    85	
    86	## Self-check
    87	
    88	- [ ] Properties are compositor-safe, or an intentional alternative has a target-device trace
    89	- [ ] Durations/easings are tokens (`--dur-*`/`--ease-*`); entrances ~200–300ms ease-out (custom curve, not linear)
    90	- [ ] `prefers-reduced-motion` path tested (motion off or cross-fade) — content never depends on it
    91	- [ ] Page reads complete with JS disabled, delayed initialization, and bfcache restore;
    92	      pending markers are added only to offscreen elements after observer ownership and cleared on `pageshow`
    93	- [ ] Rapid repeat, cancellation, stale completion, and navigation converge on the
    94	      current usable state without stale focus, status, or layers
    95	- [ ] No scroll-jacking or uncontrolled loops; flashing stays below the safety threshold
    96	- [ ] Evidence names the tokens, settled state, reduced equivalent, and replay result
    97

   104	- Exclude: Do not add motion without a user-facing purpose. (+2 in contract.json)

     1	---
     2	name: data-viz
     3	description: >-
     4	  Honest, Tufte-informed web data visualization. Use for charts, dashboards,
     5	  metrics, KPI tiles, sparklines, chart accessibility, chartjunk removal,
     6	  genre choice, lie-factor checks, direct labels, data tables, and themed SVG/HTML.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Data Viz (honest, web-native, Tufte-informed)
    10	
    11	Make web-native charts whose marks map honestly to data, fit the comparison task, and
    12	remain tokenized, responsive, and accessible. Use specialist Tufte tooling when present.
    13	
    14	## The decision order
    15	
    16	1. State the comparison question, source/grain, missingness, and uncertainty.
    17	2. Prefer a table when exact lookup matters and the value set is small; prefer a chart
    18	   when shape, change, or comparison is the task.
    19	3. Use honest encoding (zero-baseline bars; 1-D length/position; lie factor ≈1), direct
    20	   labels, and no non-data ink.
    21	4. Provide tokenized responsive contrast-safe chart/table parity; interactive data is keyboard reachable.
    22	
    23	## Data-quality gate: decide what the marks are allowed to claim
    24	
    25	Before choosing a genre, write down the comparison question, unit, time grain, source,
    26	and the status of every absent value. A missing month can mean **zero**, **not observed**,
    27	**suppressed**, **not applicable**, or **not yet reported**; those are different data
    28	states and must not share a mark, a line segment, or a table cell treatment.
    29	
    30	Also name what an uncertainty band means (for example, confidence interval, forecast
    31	range, or measurement error), the interval level if known, and whether all observations
    32	use the same method. If values, units, provenance, or interval meaning are absent, stop
    33	at a representation specification and data-request checklist. Do not invent a trend,
    34	interpolate a gap, or manufacture an impressive empty chart.
    35	
    36	For a sparse monthly series, prefer an observed-month table or interval strip when it
    37	makes the absences easier to read. If a line remains useful, break it across missing
    38	observations, plot uncertainty only where the estimate exists, directly label the gap,
    39	and do not let a continuous area imply unobserved continuity.
    40	
    41	## Genre selection (do this before any code)
    42	
    43	| Data shape | Use | Not |
    44	|---|---|---|
    45	| Small set; exact lookup matters | **a table** (or supertable) | a chart that slows lookup |
    46	| 1 series over time | line / **sparkline** (inline) | 3D, area-fill-as-decoration |
    47	| Few categories, one value each | **bar from zero**, direct-labeled | pie (hard to compare angles) |
    48	| Part-to-whole, 2–4 parts | bar or stacked bar; a number + bar | pie/donut (avoid >3 slices) |
    49	| Many series / facets | **small multiples** (repeat one shrunken chart, shared scale) | one overplotted spaghetti chart |
    50	| Distribution of groups | quartile/box or strip plot | bar-of-averages (hides spread) |
    51	| Two variables | scatter with a **range frame** | bordered box + gridlines |
    52	| Trend per row in a table | inline **sparklines** | a separate chart page |
    53	| Geographic + multivariate | map / Minard-style layered | a generic choropleth rainbow |
    54	
    55	**Default-challenge rule:** if your pick is the unprompted default (pie, 3-color bar,
    56	spaghetti line), either justify why the alternatives lose, or reach for the stronger
    57	Tufte move (table, small multiples, sparkline, range frame).
    58	
    59	### Refuse misleading requests with a safer replacement
    60	
    61	Refuse a dramatic 3-D chart because depth, perspective, and occlusion encode values the
    62	data does not contain—not because “3-D is bad.” State the comparison it would distort,
    63	then offer the smallest honest alternative: a direct-labeled interval strip, observed-
    64	month table, shared-scale small multiple, or zero-baseline bar as appropriate. The
    65	replacement must preserve the requested decision task where the data supports it.
    66	
    67	## Non-negotiables (checkable)
    68	
    69	- **Table for lookup; chart for shape.** A 12-month trend may deserve a line despite
    70	  having only 12 values; a 40-cell audit may still belong in a sortable table.
    71	- **Bars start at zero when length encodes magnitude.** For floating ranges or deltas,
    72	  use an explicit interval/difference encoding instead of a conventional truncated bar.
    73	- **Use position or length for quantitative comparison.** Do not exaggerate one value
    74	  with area or volume. Redundant color-plus-shape, label, or pattern encoding is welcome
    75	  when it improves identification or accessibility; redundant channels must agree.
    76	- **Lie factor ≈ 1.0** (visual-change% ÷ data-change%, acceptable 0.95–1.05). Check it
    77	  whenever a proportion looks dramatic.
    78	- **Direct labels, not a legend** where feasible — label the line end / the bar.
    79	- **Kill chartjunk:** no gridlines heavier than the data, no moiré fills/gradients
    80	  carrying no data, no drop shadows, no decorative imagery, no borders that aren't data.
    81	- **Limit simultaneous hues to what can be distinguished; color is never the only
    82	  channel.** Distinguish series by position,
    83	  direct label, or pattern too (color-blind + WCAG 1.4.1). Series colors come from
    84	  `color-system` tokens and must hit ≥3:1 vs background and vs adjacent series.
    85	- **Works in dark mode** (axes/text re-checked for contrast on the dark surface).
    86	- **Responsive** — SVG `viewBox`/fluid width, no fixed px that overflow at 320px.
    87	- **Every chart has a text equivalent** — a caption stating the takeaway, plus an
    88	  accessible data table (visually-hidden or toggle) for screen readers. A chart is
    89	  not done until a non-sighted user can get the numbers.
    90	
    91	## Premium restraint
    92	
    93	Use existing type, spacing, surface, and color tokens: hierarchy, alignment, and one
    94	purposeful accent can add craft; decoration must never compete with data.
    95	
    96	## Quick-start
    97	
    98	Use `assets/chart-starter.html` for the line, sparkline, and table-first skeleton. Keep
    99	the invariants here: range-frame axis, direct end labels, caption takeaway, and a real
   100	table equivalent.
   101	
   102	## Reference files
   103	
   104	- `references/tufte-and-genres.md` — data-ink, lie factor, and genre playbook.
   105	- `references/web-and-a11y.md` — tokens, responsive SVG, and accessible parity.
   106	- `references/decision-records.md` — novel-case ADR rules.
   107	
   108	## Decision order and evidence
   109	
   110	Chart and accessible table expose the same observed value, unit, uncertainty, missingness
   111	reason, source, and filter; a caption states what can and cannot be concluded. Record
   112	data quality, selected/refused genre, encoding, parity, and rendered evidence.
   113	
   114	## Self-check (before shipping a chart)
   115	
   116	1. Table/genre fits the comparison—not a default chart?
   117	2. Zero bars, 1-D encoding, lie factor ≈1, direct labels/range frame, no chartjunk?
   118	3. Tokenized ≤5-hue non-color encoding, ≥3:1 contrast, dark mode, and 320px behavior?
   119	4. Caption and table parity retain source, unit, uncertainty, filters, and missingness;
   120	   gaps are not false continuity?
   121	
   122	## How to deliver
   123	
   124	State the genre and why, report a material lie factor, and include table parity, caption, contrast, and theming evidence. Use specialist Tufte tooling only when available.
   125	
   126	## Provenance
   127	
   128	This is an independent web-and-accessibility synthesis of Tufte principles; use
   129	specialist assessment/rendering tools when available.
   130

     2	name: art-direction
     3	description: >-
     4	  Imagery, illustration, and iconography direction for web UI. Use for hero
     5	  images, photo treatment, illustration style, icon sets, favicons, OG/social
     6	  cards, decorative graphics, image-shaped holes, and removing generic AI imagery.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Art Direction
    10	
    11	Art direction gives imagery a job in the interface: orient, explain, establish trust, or create a controlled emotional cue. It is not a list of visual adjectives.
    12	
    13	## Set the visual job before selecting assets
    14	
    15	Write a one-line stance: audience, moment, desired response, and what imagery must communicate that copy cannot. Then choose one primary treatment and one fallback. A treatment can be documentary detail, diagrammatic illustration, editorial crop, icon system, or deliberately no image.
    16	
    17	| Decision | Specify | Reject when |
    18	| --- | --- | --- |
    19	| Subject | people, object, place, or abstract concept | it repeats the headline without adding evidence |
    20	| Point of view | distance, angle, energy, inclusion | it turns a serious task into decoration |
    21	| Composition | focal zone, text-safe area, responsive crop | the message disappears at narrow widths |
    22	| Surface | palette, texture, line weight, lighting | it conflicts with product semantic states |
    23	| Rights | source, license, attribution, expiry | ownership or reuse is unknown |
    24	
    25	## Turn the stance into an asset contract
    26	
    27	For each proposed asset, record its source, allowed use, focal subject, crop anchors for wide and narrow layouts, overlay treatment, and alt decision. If no approved library exists, deliver a search brief or commissioning brief—not fabricated filenames or claimed licenses.
    28	
    29	- Make the narrow crop independently legible; do not assume a desktop crop merely shrinks.
    30	- Reserve readable text space in the composition before applying an overlay.
    31	- Prefer a coherent series over unrelated stock images. Inclusion must be credible to the subject and setting.
    32	- Use decorative alt only when removing the image loses no task-relevant information. Otherwise state the information in concise alt text or adjacent copy.
    33	
    34	## Prove it in the rendered layout
    35	
    36	Show one wide and one narrow composition with the real headline, navigation, and state colors present. Check that focal content survives crops, overlays preserve text contrast, repeated cards do not create visual noise, and icons remain distinguishable at their rendered size.
    37	
    38	Deliver a small treatment board with: stance, primary/fallback treatment, two representative assets or search terms, crop notes, rights status, alt strategy, and the reject criterion. Treat source URLs, licenses, and approval status as evidence—not ornamental credits.
    39	
    40	Use [decision records](references/decision-records.md) when competing treatments require a documented trade-off.
    41	
    42	## Favicons and app icons
    43	
    44	Ship an SVG favicon primary (`<link rel="icon" href="/favicon.svg" type="image/svg+xml">`)
    45	with a PNG fallback. Derive the mark from the committed stance; test legibility at 16×16
    46	actual pixels — a single bold glyph outperforms a detailed logo. Apple Touch Icon (180),
    47	manifest icons (192/512), and OG preview are separate deliverables with their own crop and
    48	contrast needs. See [patterns](references/patterns.md) for worked examples.
    49	
    50	## Ship check
    51	
    52	- [ ] The treatment explains a product or content need that copy alone cannot.
    53	- [ ] Every selected image has source, rights, crop, and alt decisions.
    54	- [ ] The narrow composition and text-over-image state have been reviewed.
    55	- [ ] A missing or unlicensed asset is represented as a brief, not an invented deliverable.
    56

    59	
    60	Canonical detail: [contract.json](contract.json).
    61	
    62	- Route: A product needs a committed imagery, illustration, or iconography system with source and rights decisions.; avoid: The request is only a color, type, or layout decision.
    63	- Exclude: Do not use placeholder imagery when the brief requires a real asset plan. (+1 in contract.json)
    64	- Stop / handoff: Stop when source or rights constraints cannot be verified. (+1 in contract.json); receives [design-system-interview, improve-existing-website, tasteroll] -> sends [responsive-layout, a11y-pass, deslop-ui, tastecheck-pass]
    65	- Output: source-aware visual treatment and asset plan

     1	---
     2	name: a11y-pass
     3	description: >-
     4	  WCAG 2.2 AA fix pass for web UI. Use before shipping, or for requests about
     5	  accessibility, a11y, keyboard navigation, screen readers, contrast, labels,
     6	  focus, landmarks, target size, reduced motion, or ARIA.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Accessibility Pass (WCAG 2.2 AA)
    10	
    11	Accessibility is whether a person can finish a real task without an avoidable barrier. Repair the failing path, then prove the repair in the rendered interface.
    12	
    13	## Triage one rendered user path
    14	
    15	Write one path before opening a checker: **entry → action → result/recovery**. Name the user-facing outcome, the affected UI, and the smallest viewport or assistive setup that can expose the failure.
    16	
    17	| Path point | Inspect | Repair when it fails |
    18	| --- | --- | --- |
    19	| Entry | landmark, page title, reading order, skip link | give the page a named, reachable start |
    20	| Action | visible focus, native semantics, keyboard order, target size | repair the control before adding ARIA |
    21	| Change | errors, loading, dialogs, live updates, motion | announce the meaningful change without stealing focus |
    22	| Recovery | error copy, focus destination, retained input, retry | return the person to a specific next action |
    23	| Layout | 200% and 400% zoom, reflow, narrow viewport | remove horizontal loss and clipped controls |
    24	
    25	Start with native HTML. Use ARIA only to express a relationship or live behavior native elements cannot supply. Never use a role, audit score, or automated pass as proof that the path works.
    26	
    27	## Prove the path in the interface
    28	
    29	1. Tab forward and backward from the browser chrome through completion. Record focus order, visible indicator, and any trap.
    30	2. Trigger the failure state with the keyboard. Confirm focus stays purposeful, the error is programmatically associated, and entered data survives.
    31	3. Read the affected region with a screen reader or inspect its computed name, role, value, state, and description. Check the update is announced once.
    32	4. Measure the final rendered foreground/background pair and test reflow at 200% and 400%. Include text over imagery and disabled-looking controls if they carry meaning.
    33	5. Repeat with reduced motion when the interaction animates. The non-motion path must still expose progress and completion.
    34	
    35	A screenshot can support a claim; it cannot prove keyboard order or announcements. An automated audit can find candidates; it cannot clear the path.
    36	
    37	## Report a repair ledger
    38	
    39	Use this row format for every finding:
    40	
    41	| Path / criterion | Observed failure | Repair | Evidence | Residual risk |
    42	| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
    43	| Submit → invalid email | focus lands on an unnamed summary; inline error is silent | move focus to named summary, link field error with `aria-describedby`, retain value | keyboard trace; computed accessibility tree; 400% capture | screen-reader wording needs product review |
    44	
    45	Separate a confirmed defect from a design concern. State the browser, assistive technology or inspection method, viewport/zoom, and exact reproduction step. If the artifact is unavailable, stop and ask for the rendered path rather than inventing evidence.
    46	
    47	## Delivery and handoff
    48	
    49	Prioritize blockers to task completion, then frequent-path defects, then polish. Send component lifecycle or form-state questions to the owning skill when the defect is a state-design problem rather than a WCAG repair.
    50	
    51	- [Audit method](references/audit.md)
    52	- [Keyboard and focus helper](assets/audit.js)
    53	- [Decision records](references/decision-records.md)
    54	
    55	## Ship check
    56	
    57	- [ ] A named user path has keyboard, semantic, zoom/reflow, and dynamic-state evidence.
    58	- [ ] Every finding names a concrete control or region, repair, and verification result.
    59	- [ ] Automated checks are labeled as discovery, not sign-off.
    60	- [ ] Focus, announcements, errors, and reduced-motion behavior have an explicit owner.
    61	
    62	## Provenance — standard, not opinion
    63	
    64	Grounded in the W3C Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG) 2.2, Level AA — the conformance target named throughout this skill (keyboard, focus, contrast, reflow, target size, reduced motion, error identification, status announcements). Independent Kyanite Labs synthesis of the standard's success criteria, credited — not copied. WCAG 2.2 is a W3C Recommendation; its success criteria are factual conformance requirements. Hand cognitive-load concerns to `cognitive-a11y` (COGA).
    65

    72	- Exclude: Do not self-attest a fix without browser or source evidence. (+1 in contract.json)

    74	- Output: prioritized WCAG repair ledger with measured verification
    75	- Evidence: `table_with_evidence` with `status`, `reason`, `remediation`, `evidence`, `provenance`.

     2	name: cognitive-a11y
     3	description: >-
     4	  Cognitive accessibility pass for ADHD, autism, dyslexia, and neurodivergent

     8	
     9	# Cognitive Accessibility (ADHD · autism · dyslexia · neurodivergence)
    10	
    11	`a11y-pass` covers mechanical WCAG defects. This pass asks whether someone can read,
    12	understand, remember, and finish the task without avoidable cognitive cost. Profile
    13	patterns are lenses for friction, never diagnoses or universal rules.
    14	
    15	## The non-negotiables (help every profile)
    16	
    17	- **Plain and structured:** literal, front-loaded language; short chunks; summaries,
    18	  visible steps, and progress for long work.
    19	- **Predictable and low-memory:** consistent labels/help; no surprise changes; carry
    20	  forward information instead of requiring recall.
    21	- **Calm and readable:** controllable motion, no sensory overload, readable ragged-left
    22	  type (about 1.5 line-height and 45–75ch), and no justified/all-caps blocks.
    23	- **Forgiving:** explain fixes, preserve work, allow pause/extension, and confirm or
    24	  undo destructive actions.
    25	
    26	## Profile lenses
    27	
    28	Use `references/profiles.md` for detail. ADHD highlights distraction, progress, and
    29	save/resume; autism highlights predictability and sensory control; dyslexia highlights
    30	reading support and alternatives to text-only cues. Apply the shared rules to every
    31	user, including kind, non-judgmental recovery.
    32	
    33	## How to use this skill
    34	
    35	### Map friction before profiles
    36	
    37	Trace one real task from first read through interruption/error and recovery. Record the
    38	observed friction and demand, a non-diagnostic profile hypothesis, the repair, and a
    39	task-path observation that proves start, correction, or resume became easier. Then make
    40	purpose and next action skimmable; externalize memory and progress; preserve work and
    41	time control; and offer motion, density, and reading support without changing meaning.
    42	
    43	## Reference files
    44	
    45	- `references/profiles.md` — profile-specific barriers and repairs.
    46	- `references/patterns.md` — checkable cross-cutting patterns and examples.
    47	- `references/decision-records.md` — meta-patterns + ADR rules for novel cases.
    48	
    49	## Self-check (before claiming cognitively accessible)
    50	
    51	- Plain, chunked, predictable, low-memory content with one clear next action?
    52	- Calm, controllable sensory/readability conditions and kind, recoverable errors?
    53	- A real path separates observed friction from hypothesis and proves start, recovery, or resume?
    54	
    55	## How to deliver
    56	
    57	Name the profiles and concrete moves. This is the cognitive layer; hand technical/WCAG defects to `a11y-pass`.
    58	
    59	## Provenance — principle, not property
    60	Grounded in the W3C Cognitive and Learning Disabilities Accessibility Task Force (COGA)
    61	"Making Content Usable for People with Cognitive and Learning Disabilities" and
    62	established neurodivergent-design practice. Independent synthesis, credited — not copied.
    63

     1	---
     2	name: i18n-ready
     3	description: >-
     4	  Multilingual-resilient web UI, English/Spanish first-class. Use for
     5	  localization, translation, text expansion, lang attributes, logical
     6	  properties, RTL readiness, locale formats, bilingual copy tells, hreflang,
     7	  and language toggles.
     8	---
     9	
    10	# i18n-Ready (bilingual by design, not by patch)
    11	
    12	Design for the longest honest string, then translate meaning—not words. English/Spanish
    13	is the worked pair; the structural method generalizes.
    14	
    15	## Make a locale contract before translating or resizing
    16	
    17	Record a locale contract before resizing or translating:
    18	
    19	| Boundary | Required decision/evidence |
    20	| --- | --- |
    21	| locale/reader | concrete BCP 47 tag, fallback/owner, formality and regional vocabulary |
    22	| content | whole-message keys, variables/plurals, protected legal/safety language |
    23	| mechanics | formats and data source for date/time zone/number/currency/address/name |
    24	| stress/direction | longest approved fixture by surface; current `dir`, RTL/bidi/logical-property path |
    25	
    26	Never invent locale, idiom, translation, or formality; pending source/approval means
    27	structural readiness only.
    28	
    29	## The decision order
    30	
    31	1. Set `<html lang>` and inline `lang` switches.
    32	2. Test the longest approved fixture; text containers/content-sized controls avoid fixed
    33	   widths. Intentional truncation exposes reachable full text.
    34	3. Use logical inline/block properties for RTL readiness, and `Intl` with real locales
    35	   for all mechanical formats.
    36	4. Re-express native voice/idiom/formality (including tú/usted); do not calque.
    37	5. When the product offers an in-product language choice, self-label and persist the
    38	   control and update `lang`; provide `hreflang` and per-locale metadata for indexed
    39	   locale routes where applicable.
    40	
    41	## Non-negotiables
    42	
    43	- Correct language attributes, longest-string-safe layout, and logical CSS—no text images
    44	  or concatenated sentence fragments.
    45	- Explicit-locale `Intl`; native Spanish with consistent formality, accents/ñ/¿¡, and
    46	  `cognitive-a11y` pass per locale.
    47	
    48	## Verify meaning, layout, and mechanics separately
    49	
    50	Keep three evidence lanes distinct: **meaning** (native/approved intent, formality,
    51	idiom, plural/protected wording); **layout** (longest fixtures in real views/states;
    52	pseudo-localization finds width defects only); **mechanical** (`Intl`, time zones,
    53	currencies, inputs, `lang`/`dir`, logical properties, toggle persistence). A date changing
    54	calendar day is a data defect.
    55	
    56	## Repair order and evidence
    57	
    58	Require locale contract and approved fixtures first. Repair message boundaries, formats,
    59	keys, wrapping, logical CSS/bidi, and native voice, then record separate three-lane checks
    60	per locale/surface with reviewer/source authority.
    61	
    62	## Self-check (per language, before shipping)
    63	
    64	1. Correct `lang`, logical CSS, longest-fixture layout, and no fragmented/image text?
    65	2. Explicit-locale `Intl`, native/formality-safe copy, and per-language cognitive pass?
    66	3. Honest persisted toggle/metadata where applicable, with separate meaning/layout/mechanical proof?
    67	
    68	## How to deliver
    69	
    70	Deliver locale readiness: tag/fallback, formality source, longest fixture, three-lane
    71	evidence, bidi/format risk, and owner. Keep locales in `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` and hand off
    72	voice/type/accessibility work.
    73	
    74	## Reference files
    75	
    76	- `references/decision-records.md` — meta-patterns + ADR rules for novel cases.
    77	- `assets/locale-fixtures.json` — EN/ES message keys, format examples, and longest-string stress fixtures.
    78

     1	---
     2	name: humanize-copy
     3	description: >-
     4	  Humanize prose by removing LLM tells. Use for landing copy, docs, READMEs,
     5	  emails, UI microcopy, release notes, social posts, or requests like humanize,
     6	  less robotic, remove AI tells, punch up, or make this sound human.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Humanize Copy
    10	
    11	Human copy sounds like someone meant it for this reader, in this moment. Keep the facts;
    12	replace formula with consequence, stance, and a cadence the speaker could sustain aloud.
    13	
    14	## Establish the speaking situation before editing
    15	
    16	Do not synonym-swap detached text. Build a voice brief from source, reader job, protected
    17	language, defensible claim, and situational register. If the intended author is a real
    18	person, use supplied or approved samples; never infer a private identity from stereotypes.
    19	
    20	| Voice brief field | Evidence to use |
    21	| --- | --- |
    22	| Speaker and audience | Named role, relationship, and reader context from the request/source |
    23	| Reader job | Decision, action, reassurance, or understanding the text must support |
    24	| Defensible claim | Source fact, direct quote, measurement, or explicitly marked opinion |
    25	| Protected language | Legal, safety, product, customer, or brand wording that cannot be casually altered |
    26	| Register boundary | Degree of formality, humor, intimacy, and certainty the situation can bear |
    27	
    28	Without facts/voice authority, change only clarity, order, and cadence; never invent
    29	claims, metrics, anecdotes, testimony, or personal voice.
    30	
    31	## Edit in three passes
    32	
    33	1. **Source:** mark protected meaning and the factual authority behind every claim.
    34	2. **Stance:** decide what the speaker can say plainly, then lead with the reader's
    35	   consequence, decision, or next action.
    36	3. **Shape:** cut the wrapper, restore causal order, and tune sentence length and register.
    37	
    38	Compare the revision to the source; apply the portable floor in
    39	`references/writing-constitution.md`, then use the kill list and rhythm reference as
    40	detectors after meaning is settled.
    41	
    42	## Detect formula before replacing words
    43	
    44	The kill list is a detector, not a ban. Inspect abstract buzzwords, ceremonial hooks,
    45	manufactured contrast, repeated rule-of-three or em-dash cadence, hedges, and generic
    46	metaphor. Preserve necessary technical, legal, and brand terms. When a detector fires,
    47	repair the underlying mechanism: name the subject, state the consequence, or remove the
    48	sentence. A fancier synonym is not a repair.
    49	
    50	## Worked transformation
    51	
    52	**Source facts:** appointments are booked online; technicians give a two-hour arrival
    53	window; customers receive a text before arrival.
    54	
    55	**Before:** “Experience seamless repair solutions designed to get your day back on track.”
    56	
    57	**After:** “Book online. We’ll give you a two-hour window and text before the technician
    58	arrives.”
    59	
    60	The revision earns its confidence from the source. It replaces an unsupported emotional
    61	promise with the decision, the timing, and the useful reassurance. This is the target:
    62	not universally casual copy, but language whose specificity and rhythm fit its authority.
    63	
    64	## Decision order and evidence
    65	
    66	Build the brief and fact ledger, then revise stance, order, precision, and cadence without
    67	changing facts or safety meaning. Record source authority for every new specificity.
    68	
    69	## Self-check (run before claiming "humanized")
    70	
    71	1. Read-aloud test, direct opening, and no formulaic cadence, hedge, or thesaurus-salad?
    72	2. Source-backed specifics and bounded claims—not competitor-generic copy or invented texture?
    73	3. Register fits this reader, task, and consequence?
    74	4. Writing rules applied and their IDs recorded in the evidence?
    75	
    76	## Reference files
    77	
    78	- `references/kill-list.md` — full detector list and examples.
    79	- `references/rhythm-and-voice.md` — structure, stance, and cadence.
    80	- `references/decision-records.md` — novel-case ADR rules.
    81	- `references/writing-constitution.md` — portable information-architecture floor.
    82	
    83	## How to deliver
    84	
    85	Deliver the revised copy first when that is what the user asked for. Follow with the voice
    86	brief and 2–5 high-leverage changes by mechanism when rationale is useful. Preserve facts
    87	and register; pair non-English work with `i18n-ready`.
    88	
    89	## Information-architecture gate
    90	
    91	Apply the bundled Writing Constitution floor (`references/writing-constitution.md`):
    92	old-before-new, stress position, anchor/linking/umbrella/preview, and exact key-term repetition.
    93	Run the three-pass audit and record rule IDs. This reference is portable; do not use a
    94	workstation-local copy. Echo argument terms; remove formulaic repetition.
    95

   100	
   101	- Route: UI or product copy is factually correct but generic, hedged, clichéd, or unlike the intended author voice.; avoid: The request requires changing product facts, legal meaning, or interaction behavior.
   102	- Exclude: Do not invent claims, testimonials, or product facts. (+2 in contract.json)
   103	- Stop / handoff: Stop when source facts or approval constraints are missing. (+1 in contract.json); receives [cognitive-a11y, design-system-interview, deslop-ui, empty-states, improve-existing-website, tasteroll] -> sends [form-ux, i18n-ready, tastecheck-pass]

     1	---
     2	name: tasteroll
     3	description: >-
     4	  Rapid prototyping through constrained randomness. Audits what exists, fixes
     5	  what is broken, generates fresh design candidates from context, then rolls
     6	  the dice between valid alternatives. Lock what works, re-roll what doesn't,
     7	  converge on a direction. Use for design exploration, rapid prototyping, or
     8	  when a user wants surprise within guardrails.
     9	---
    10	
    11	# Tasteroll (roll for taste, keep what sticks)
    12	
    13	Most design generators either ask you everything (the full interview) or nothing
    14	(press a button, hope for the best). Tasteroll is the middle path: it audits
    15	what exists, asks at most three questions, fixes what is broken, then rolls
    16	between valid alternatives. Lock what works, re-roll what doesn't.
    17	
    18	The thesis: **constraints produce taste, even when the choices are random** —
    19	but only if the constraints come from the actual project, not a fixed list.
    20	
    21	## The pipeline
    22	
    23	```
    24	1. AUDIT     → scan what exists → mandatory findings (craft-only)
    25	2. INTAKE    → infer from context/repo OR 3-question mini-interview
    26	3. FIX       → resolve all findings (seed-independent, non-negotiable)
    27	4. GENERATE  → fresh candidates for open dimensions (context-aware)
    28	5. ROLL      → seeded pick between valid alternatives
    29	6. LOCK      → keep what works, re-roll what doesn't, converge
    30	7. GATE      → relaxed gate (readable + WCAG AA + self-contained + inert)
    31	```
    32	
    33	## 1. Audit (findings are mandatory fixes)
    34	
    35	Scan rendered surfaces, source CSS, repo files, and content structure. Findings
    36	are **non-negotiable** — they get resolved before the dice touch anything,
    37	regardless of seed. The audit scope is **craft-only**:
    38	
    39	- **Contrast**: body < 4.5:1 or UI < 3:1 on actual surfaces
    40	- **Spacing**: no scale, arbitrary margins with no token system
    41	- **States**: interactive elements missing hover/focus/active/disabled
    42	- **Tells**: named AI slop patterns present (gradient, centered hero, 3 cards)
    43	- **Accessibility**: missing alt, unlabeled controls, broken heading order
    44	- **Structure**: missing main landmark, no skip link, no focus-visible
    45	
    46	Findings outside craft scope (bad copy, content gaps, broken features) are
    47	**flagged and handed off** — not fixed by tasteroll.
    48	
    49	A finding can be overridden with a single-word "preserve" if the low contrast
    50	or missing state is an intentional aesthetic choice. Without an override, every
    51	finding is resolved.
    52	
    53	For greenfield (nothing to audit), this phase scans the brief and content for
    54	contradictions, missing scope, and vague direction instead of rendered surfaces.
    55	
    56	## 2. Intake (context for candidate generation)
    57	
    58	Two paths, chosen by signal availability:
    59	
    60	**Infer** (default): read the conversation so far + scan repo files (README,
    61	existing CSS, package.json, content). If there is enough signal to know what
    62	the product is and who it is for, skip the questions.
    63	
    64	**Mini-interview** (fallback): three questions when inference is thin:
    65	
    66	1. **What are you building?** → unlocks structure, density, IA
    67	2. **Who is it for?** → unlocks personality, accessibility posture, complexity
    68	3. **One word for how it should feel?** → unlocks aesthetic, accent direction
    69	
    70	Each question unlocks different axes. Three answers give real coverage without
    71	the weight of the full 11-field `design-system-interview`.
    72	
    73	## 3. Fix (deterministic, seed-independent)
    74	
    75	Every audit finding is resolved here. This step produces the same output
    76	regardless of seed — it is a floor, not a variable. The output section
    77	separates "resolved findings" (deterministic) from "rolled choices" (random).
    78	
    79	## 4. Generate (fresh candidates, context-aware)
    80	
    81	For each open design dimension (personality, aesthetic, type, color_mode,
    82	density, rhythm, signature, imagery, motion, accent), the AI generates **2–5
    83	candidate options** from the audit findings + intake context. These are not
    84	from a static file — they are produced at roll time from what the agent knows
    85	about the project.
    86	
    87	Candidates must satisfy the **compatibility rules** (see `assets/design-rails.json`):
    88	compact density pairs with metronomic rhythm; airy pairs with syncopated;
    89	brutalist aesthetics pair with restrained motion; etc. The generation step
    90	must not produce candidates that contradict each other.
    91	
    92	Candidates must also respect any **locked dimensions** from previous rolls.
    93	
    94	## 5. Roll (seeded pick)
    95	
    96	The PRNG (`assets/tasteroll-engine.js`) picks one candidate per open dimension.
    97	Same seed + same candidates → same roll, always. The seed makes rolls
    98	reproducible and shareable.
    99	
   100	If Chance (kyanitelabs/chance) is available via MCP, prefer it for multi-source
   101	entropy mixing and reproducible audit trails. The inline engine is the fallback.
   102	
   103	## 6. Lock and re-roll (progressive commitment)
   104	
   105	Three interaction modes:
   106	
   107	- **One-shot**: single roll. Take it or re-roll from scratch.
   108	- **Iterative**: roll → review → lock dimensions that work → re-roll unlocked
   109	  dimensions only → converge. Each re-roll increments the seed and excludes
   110	  already-shown directions for unlocked dimensions.
   111	- **Shotgun**: roll N seeds at once → compare side by side → pick one to
   112	  develop further.
   113	
   114	Locked dimensions and resolved findings persist across all re-rolls. Only open
   115	dimensions vary.
   116	
   117	## 7. Gate (relaxed)
   118	
   119	Tasteroll output is a personal artifact or prototype, not a shipped surface.
   120	The gate is deliberately lighter than `tastecheck-pass`:
   121	
   122	- **Readable**: body ≥ 1rem, measure 58–75ch, line-height ≥ 1.5
   123	- **WCAG AA**: body ≥ 4.5:1, UI ≥ 3:1 (verified on the rolled surfaces)
   124	- **Self-contained**: no external requests in exports
   125	- **Inert**: no scripts in exported HTML
   126	- **Keyboard-reachable**: all interactive elements focusable with visible focus
   127	
   128	If the output will be a **shipped public surface**, escalate to `tastecheck-pass`.
   129	
   130	## The design rails
   131	
   132	The hard constraints live in `assets/design-rails.json`. These define what
   133	dimensions exist, what values are always valid (measure 58–75ch, line-height
   134	1.5–1.8, one accent max, corner radius from {0,2,4,6}), and the compatibility
   135	rules between dimensions. Any roll that violates a rail fails the gate.
   136	
   137	The rails file does NOT contain option lists. Options are generated fresh.
   138	
   139	## What makes this different
   140	
   141	- **v0 / LLM generators**: no seed, no audit, no rules, no reproducibility
   142	- **Coolors**: random colors, no system, no structure, no gate
   143	- **Tasteroll**: audit-driven fixes + context-aware candidates + seeded pick +
   144	  lock/reroll + complete design system + quality gate
   145	
   146	## Reference files
   147	
   148	- `assets/design-rails.json` — dimensions, hard constraints, audit scope,
   149	  intake questions, compatibility rules.
   150	- `assets/tasteroll-engine.js` — pasteable xoshiro128++ PRNG with lock, reroll,
   151	  shotgun, and exclude operations.
   152	
   153	## How to deliver
   154	
   155	Deliver: the seed, the resolved findings (deterministic), the rolled choices
   156	(seed-dependent), the derived DESIGN-SYSTEM.md, and the relaxed-gate evidence.
   157	Hand off to downstream skills (color-system, web-typography, spacing-system,
   158	theming, etc.) with the same handoff fields as `design-system-interview`.
   159	
   160	If the user says "roll again," increment the seed — never silently re-roll with
   161	the same seed and present different answers.
   162	
   163	## Completion evidence
   164	
   165	| Status | Reason | Remediation | Evidence | Provenance |
   166	| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   167	|  | audit — findings and their resolution status |  |  |  |
   168	|  | intake — inference result or mini-interview answers |  |  |  |
   169	|  | fixes — resolved findings (seed-independent) |  |  |  |
   170	|  | roll — seed, locked dimensions, rolled choices |  |  |  |
   171	|  | gate — relaxed-gate contrast, readability, self-containment |  |  |  |
   172	
   173	<!-- contract:v1:start -->
   174	## Contract (generated)
   175	
   176	Canonical detail: [contract.json](contract.json).
   177	
   178	- Route: Rapid design exploration or surprise direction without a full interview (+2 in contract.json); avoid: The user wants to commit to a specific direction by hand (+2 in contract.json)
   179	- Exclude: Do not roll outside the design rails or bypass the audit findings (+3 in contract.json)
   180	- Stop / handoff: Stop when the output will be a shipped public surface — escalate to tastecheck-pass strict gate (+1 in contract.json); receives [design-system-interview, improve-existing-website] -> sends [color-system, web-typography, spacing-system, theming, responsive-layout, art-direction, micro-motion, component-states, data-viz, empty-states, form-ux, humanize-copy]
   181	- Output: A seeded DESIGN-SYSTEM.md with resolved findings, rolled choices, and gate evidence
   182	- Evidence: `table_with_evidence` with `status`, `reason`, `remediation`, `evidence`, `provenance`.
   183	<!-- contract:v1:end -->

    23	  ["<!-- release-status:v1:start -->", "<!-- release-status:v1:end -->"],

    27	function normalizePublicStatusSurface(path, content) {
    28	  if (!publicStatusSurfaces.has(path)) return content;
    29	  let normalized = content.toString("utf8");
    30	  for (const [start, end] of publicStatusMarkers) {
    31	    const startIndex = normalized.indexOf(start);
    32	    const endIndex = normalized.indexOf(end, startIndex + start.length);
    33	    if (startIndex !== -1 && endIndex !== -1) {
    34	      normalized = `${normalized.slice(0, startIndex)}${start}${normalized.slice(endIndex)}`;
    35	    }
    36	  }
    37	  return Buffer.from(normalized, "utf8");
    38	}
    39	
    40	export function isExcludedReceiptPath(path) {
    41	  const normalized = path.replaceAll("\\", "/");
    42	  return excludedReceipts.has(normalized)
    43	    || (normalized.startsWith("evals/receipts/v1/") && !normalized.startsWith("evals/receipts/v1/immutable/"));
    44	}

    46	export function isExcludedSourcePath(path) {
    47	  const normalized = path.replaceAll("\\", "/");
    48	  return isExcludedReceiptPath(normalized) || normalized.startsWith("_retrofit-");
    49	}
